denotes the group of morphisms from
a
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}
to a
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{Q}}
in the category

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules
[
67
, Section 3.1.2]
.

Proposition
.

(a)
The following two conditions on a coring
{\mathcal{C}}
are
equivalent:

•

the category of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules is abelian and
the forgetful functor
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\allowbreak\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is exact;

•

the coring
{\mathcal{C}}
is a flat right
A
-module.

(b)
The following two conditions on a coring
{\mathcal{C}}
are
equivalent:

•

the category of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules is abelian and
the forgetful functor
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is exact;

•

the coring
{\mathcal{C}}
is a projective left
A
-module.

Proof.

One defines a
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule or
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule structure on
the kernel and cokernel of any morphism of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules or
left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules computed in the category of abelian
groups/left
A
-modules, assuming respectively that the functor

{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}{-}\colon A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is exact (preserves kernels) or
the functor
\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},{-})\colon A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is exact
(preserves cokernels).
This allows to show that the second condition implies the first
one in either part (a) or (b).

To prove the converse implication in part (a), notice that
the functor
{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}{-}\colon\allowbreak A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is
the composition of the coinduction functor
A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}

and the forgetful functor
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
, the former of
which is right adjoint to the latter one.
Since any right adjoint functor between abelian categories is
left exact, one concludes that the functor
{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}{-}
is left exact
whenever the forgetful functor is exact.
Similarly, in part (b) the functor
\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},{-})\colon A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is the composition of the induction functor
A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
and the forgetful functor
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
,
the former of which is left adjoint to the latter one.
Since any left adjoint functor is right exact, the functor

\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},{-})
is right exact whenever the forgetful functor is
exact.
∎

Generally speaking, the cokernels of arbitrary morphisms exist
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
and are preserved by the forgetful functor

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
, but the kernels in
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
may be
problematic when
{\mathcal{C}}
is not a flat right
A
-module.
Similarly, the kernels of arbitrary morphisms exist in

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
and are preserved by the forgeftul functor

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
, but the cokernels in
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
may be
problematic when
{\mathcal{C}}
is not a projective left
A
-module.
Counterexamples showing that the categories
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
and

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
are
not
abelian in general can be found
in
[
70
, Example B.1.1]
.

Assume that the coring
{\mathcal{C}}
is a flat right
A
-module; then,
according to Proposition, the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
is abelian
and the forgetful functor
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is exact.
Both the infinite direct sums and infinite products exist in

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
; the infinite direct sums are exact and are preserved
by the forgetful functor.
Filtered inductive limits are exact in the category of left

{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules; so it satisfies the axioms Ab5 and Ab3*, but

not
in general Ab4*.
The category
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
also has a set of
generators
[
22
, Sections 3.13 and 18.14]
; moreover,
when
A
is a left Noetherian ring or
{\mathcal{C}}
is a projective right

A
-module, every left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule is the union of its
subcomodules that are finitely generated as

A
-modules
[
22
, Sections 18.16 and 19.12]
.

When
{\mathcal{C}}
is a projective right
A
-module (and also under some
weaker assumptions), the
A
-
A
-bimodule
{\mathfrak{A}}=\Hom_{A^{\mathrm{op}}}({\mathcal{C}},A)

can be endowed with a topological ring structure (with a base of
neighborhoods of zero formed by open left ideals) such that
the category of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules is isomorphic to the category
of discrete left
{\mathfrak{A}}
-modules,
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\simeq{\mathfrak{A}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--discr}}}

[
90
, Proposition 10.5 and Remark 10.6]
.
This provides another point of view on the categorical properties
of the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
.

The coaction map
\nu\colon{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{M}}
embeds every left

{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
as a subcomodule into the coinduced

{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{M}}
.
Infinite products of coinduced
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules are computed
by the rule
\prod_{\alpha}{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}V_{\alpha}={\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}\prod_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}

[
22
, Section 18.13]
; to compute the product of an arbitrary
family of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules, one can present them as the kernels
of morphisms of coinduced
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules and use the fact
that infinite products always commute with
the kernels
[
67
, Section 1.1.2]
.
There are enough injective objects in the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
;
a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule is injective if and only if it is isomorphic
to a direct summand of a
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}J
coinduced from
an injective left
A
-module
J
(see
[
22
, Section 18.19]

or
[
67
, Section 5.1.5]
).

Assume that the coring
{\mathcal{C}}
is a projective left
A
-module; then,
according to Proposition, the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
is abelian and
the forgetful functor
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}\longrightarrow A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is exact.
Both the infinite direct sums and infinite products exist in

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
; the infinite products are exact and are preserved by
the forgetful functor.
So the category of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules satisfies the axioms
Ab3 and Ab4*, but
not
in general Ab4 or Ab5*.

The contraaction map
\pi\colon\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathfrak{P}})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}
presents every
left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule as a quotient contramodule of the induced

{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathfrak{P}})
.
Infinite direct sums of induced
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules are computed
by the rule
\bigoplus_{\alpha}\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},V_{\alpha})=\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},\medspace\bigoplus_{\alpha}V_{\alpha})
; to compute the direct sum of
an arbitrary family of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules, one can present
them as the cokernels of morphisms of induced
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules
and use the fact that infinite direct sums always commute with
the cokernels
[
67
, Section 3.1.2]
.
There are enough projective objects in
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
; a left

{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule is projective if and only if it is a direct summand
of a
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},F)
induced from a projective
left
A
-module
F

[
67
, Section 5.1.5]
.

Furthermore, under the same assumption of
{\mathcal{C}}
being a projective
left
A
-module, the
A
-
A
-bimodule
{\mathfrak{R}}=\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},A)

can be endowed with a topological ring structure (with a base of
neighborhoods of zero formed by open right ideals) such that
the category of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules is isomorphic to the category
of left
{\mathfrak{R}}
-contramodules,
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}\simeq{\mathfrak{R}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}

[
90
, Propositions 10.4 and 10.5]

(see Section
3.7
below for a further discussion).
This provides another point of view on the categorical properties
of the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
.

The discussion in the beginning of this section suggests that one
should consider, in addition to the categories of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules,
right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules, and left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules, the pairing
functors of
cotensor product
and
cohomomorphisms
acting
from those categories to the category of abelian groups.
Let us define these functors of two co/contramodule
arguments now.

The
cotensor product

{\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{M}}
of a right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{N}}

and a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
is an abelian group defined as the kernel
of the difference of the pair of maps

\nu_{\mathcal{N}}\otimes{\mathrm{id}},\ \,{\mathrm{id}}\otimes\nu_{\mathcal{M}}\colon{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{M}}\rightrightarrows{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{M}}

one of which is induced by the
{\mathcal{C}}
-coaction in
{\mathcal{N}}
and the other
one by the
{\mathcal{C}}
-coaction in
{\mathcal{M}}
.
For any right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{N}}
and any left
A
-module
V

there is a natural isomorphism of abelian groups

{\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}V)\simeq{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{A}V;

the similar formula holds for the cotensor product of a coinduced
right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule and an arbitrary left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule.
In particular, one has
{\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{C}}\simeq{\mathcal{N}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{M}}\simeq{\mathcal{M}}

(see
[
22
, Section 21]
or
[
67
, Sections 0.2.1 and 1.2.1]
).

The abelian group of
cohomomorphisms

\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathfrak{P}})
from
a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
to a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}
is
defined as the cokernel of (the difference of) the pair of maps

\Hom(\nu_{\mathcal{M}},{\mathrm{id}}),\ \,\Hom({\mathrm{id}},\pi_{\mathfrak{P}})\colon\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{M}},\medspace{\mathfrak{P}})\\
\simeq\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{M}},\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathfrak{P}}))\rightrightarrows\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathfrak{P}}).

For any left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
, any left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}
,
and any left
A
-module
V
, there are natural isomorphisms of
abelian groups

\displaystyle\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{A}V,\medspace{\mathfrak{P}})\simeq\Hom_{A}(V,{\mathfrak{P}})

\displaystyle\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}},\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{C}},V))\simeq\Hom_{A}({\mathcal{M}},V);

in particular, one has
\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathfrak{P}})\simeq{\mathfrak{P}}

[
67
, Sections 0.2.4 and 3.2.1]
.

Notice that the functor of cotensor product
\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}
over
a coring
{\mathcal{C}}
, being defined as the kernel of a morphism of
cokernels, is
neither
left
nor
right exact in general.
Similarly, the functor
\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}
, being defined as the cokernel
of a morphism of kernels, is neither left
nor
right exact
(even when all the categories involved are abelian and all
the forgetful functors are exact).

2.6.
Semicontramodules over semialgebras

The notion of a
semialgebra
over a coalgebra over a field
is dual to that of a coring in the same way as the notion of
a coalgebra over a field is dual to that of an (associative)
ring
[
2
,
21
,
66
,
67
]
.
In this section we present the related piece of theory, aiming to
define semimodules and semicontramodules over semialgebras and
interpret contramodules over topological groups as semicontramodules
over certain semialgebras, as it was promised in
Section
1.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a (coassociative) coalgebra (with counit) over a field
k
.
In addition to the definitions of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules, right

{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules and left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules given in
Section
1.1
and then repeated, in the greater
generality of a coring
{\mathcal{C}}
, in the previous Section
2.5
,
we will also need the definition of a
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{C}}
-bicomodule.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be another coalgebra over
k
.
A
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{D}}
-bicomodule

{\mathcal{K}}
is a
k
-vector space endowed
with a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule and a right
{\mathcal{D}}
-comodule structures

\nu^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}
and
\nu^{\prime\prime}\colon{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}
which
commute with each other in the following sense.
The composition of the left coaction map
\nu^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}

with the map
{\mathrm{id}}\otimes\nu^{\prime\prime}:{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}
induced
by the right coaction map
\nu^{\prime\prime}
should be equal to the composition
of the right coaction map
\nu^{\prime\prime}\colon{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}
with the map

\nu^{\prime}\otimes{\mathrm{id}}\colon{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}
induced by the left
coaction map
\nu^{\prime}
.
Equivalently, the vector space
{\mathcal{K}}
should be endowed with
a
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{D}}
-bicoaction
map
\nu\colon{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}

satisfying the coassociativity and counitality equations

(\mu_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{D}})\circ\nu=({\mathrm{id}}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\nu\otimes{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathcal{D}})\circ\nu

and
(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}})\circ\nu={\mathrm{id}}_{\mathcal{K}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}\rightrightarrows{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{D}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}

(see
[
22
, Sections 11.1 or 22.1]
or
[
67
, Sections 0.3.1
or 1.2.4]
).

Recall from the end of the previous section that the
cotensor
product

{\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{M}}
of a right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{N}}
and a left

{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
is the
k
-vector space constructed as the kernel
of (the difference of) the pair of maps

\nu_{\mathcal{N}}\otimes{\mathrm{id}},\ \,{\mathrm{id}}\otimes\nu_{\mathcal{M}}\colon{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}\rightrightarrows{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}

induced by the
{\mathcal{C}}
-coaction maps in
{\mathcal{N}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Similarly, the
k
-vector space of
cohomomorphisms
from
a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
to a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}
is
construced as the cokernel of the pair of maps

\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}},\medspace{\mathfrak{P}})\simeq\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{M}},\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathfrak{P}}))\rightrightarrows\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathfrak{P}})

one of which is induced by the
{\mathcal{C}}
-coaction in
{\mathcal{M}}
and the other
one by the
{\mathcal{C}}
-contraaction in
{\mathfrak{P}}
.
The functor of cotensor product of comodules over a coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}

over a field
k
, being defined as the kernel of a morphism of exact
functors, is left exact; while the functor of cohomomorphisms of
comodules and contramodules over
{\mathcal{C}}
, defined as the cokernel
of a morphism of exact functors, is right exact.
For any left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
, right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{N}}
, and

k
-vector space
V
, there is a natural isomorphism of
k
-vector
spaces
[
67
, Sections 0.2.4 and 3.2.2, and Proposition 3.2.3.1]

\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}},\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{N}},V))\simeq\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{M}},\medspace V),

where the
k
-vector space
\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{N}},V)
is endowed with
a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule structure as explained in
Section
1.2
.

For any three coalgebras
{\mathcal{C}}
,
{\mathcal{D}}
, and
{\mathcal{E}}
, any

{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{D}}
-bicomodule
{\mathcal{N}}
, and any
{\mathcal{D}}
-
{\mathcal{E}}
-bicomodule
{\mathcal{M}}
,
the cotensor product
{\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}
has a natural

{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{E}}
-bicomodule structure.
Furthermore, for any right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{N}}
, any

{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{D}}
-bicomodule
{\mathcal{K}}
, and any left
{\mathcal{D}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}

there is a natural associativity isomorphism

({\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{K}})\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}\simeq{\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{K}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}).

To put it simply, both the iterated cotensor products are
identified with one and the same subspace in the vector space

{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}
(cf. the beginning of
Section
2.5
).

Similarly, for any
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{D}}
-bicomodule
{\mathcal{K}}
and any left

{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}
, the space of cohomomorphisms

\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathfrak{P}})
has a natural left
{\mathcal{D}}
-contramodule structure.
One can define it by noticing that
\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathfrak{P}})
is
a quotient contramodule of the left
{\mathcal{D}}
-contramodule
\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathfrak{P}})
,
whose contramodule structure is induced by the right

{\mathcal{D}}
-comodule structure on
{\mathcal{K}}
via the construction described
in Section
1.2
.
For any
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{D}}
-bicomodule
{\mathcal{K}}
, any left
{\mathcal{D}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
,
and any left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}
, there is a natural
associativity isomorphism

\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{K}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}},\medspace{\mathfrak{P}})\simeq\Cohom_{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{M}},\medspace\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathfrak{P}})).

Both the (iterated) Cohom spaces are identified with
the quotient space of the vector space
\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}},\medspace{\mathfrak{P}})\simeq\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{M}},\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathfrak{P}}))
by one and the same vector subspace

[
67
, Sections 0.3.4 or 3.2.4]
.

In particular, it follows from these associativity isomorphisms
for a coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{D}}
that the category of
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{C}}
-bicomodules

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod--}}}{\mathcal{C}}
is an associative tensor category with respect to
the cotensor product functor
\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}
, the category of left

{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
is a left module category over

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod--}}}{\mathcal{C}}
, and the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}^{\mathsf{op}}
opposite to
the category of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodules is a right module category
over
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod--}}}{\mathcal{C}}
with respect to the cohomomorphism functor

\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}
.

A
semialgebra

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
over a coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}
over a field
k

is an associative ring object in the tensor category of

{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{C}}
-bicomodules.
In other words, it is a
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{C}}
-bicomodule endowed with
a
semimultiplication
map
{\mathbf{m}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
and
a
semiunit
map
{\mathbf{e}}\colon{\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
satisfying the following

colinearity
,
semiassociativity
and
semiunitality

equations.
First of all, the maps
{\mathbf{m}}
and
{\mathbf{e}}
must be
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{C}}
-bicomodule
morphisms.
Secondly, the compositions of the two maps
{\mathbf{m}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
and

{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathbf{m}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\rightrightarrows{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
induced by
the semimultiplication map
{\mathbf{m}}
with the semimultiplication map

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\rightrightarrows{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}

should be equal to each other,
{\mathbf{m}}\circ({\mathbf{m}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}})={\mathbf{m}}\circ({\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathbf{m}})
.
Thirdly, both the compositions of the maps
{\mathbf{e}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
and

{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathbf{e}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\rightrightarrows{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
induced by the semiunit
map
{\mathbf{e}}
with the semimultiplication map
{\mathbf{m}}

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\rightrightarrows{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}

should be equal to the identity map,
{\mathbf{m}}\circ({\mathbf{e}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}})={\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}={\mathbf{m}}\circ({\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathbf{e}})
.

A
left semimodule

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
over a semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
over
a coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}
is a module object in the left module category
of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules over the ring object
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
in
the tensor category of
{\mathcal{C}}
-
{\mathcal{C}}
-bicomodules.
In other words, it is a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule endowed with
a
left semiaction
map
{\mathbf{n}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}

satisfying the following colinearity,
semiassociativity

and
semiunitality
equations.
First of all, the map
{\mathbf{n}}
must be a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule morphism.
Secondly, the compositions of the two maps
{\mathbf{m}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
and

{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathbf{n}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\rightrightarrows{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
induced
by the semimultiplication and semiaction maps with
the semiaction map

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\rightrightarrows{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}

should be equal to each other,
{\mathbf{n}}\circ({\mathbf{m}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}})={\mathbf{n}}\circ({\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}{\mathbf{n}})
.
Thirdly, the composition of the map
{\mathbf{e}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
induced by the semiunit map
{\mathbf{e}}
with the semiaction
map
{\mathbf{n}}

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}

should be equal to the identity map,
{\mathbf{n}}\circ({\mathbf{e}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}})={\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
.
A
right semimodule

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
over
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
is a right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
endowed with a right semiaction map
{\mathbf{n}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

satisfying the similar equations

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\rightrightarrows{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}.

These definitions can be found in
[
2
, Sections 2.3 and 6.1]
,

[
21
, Section 6]
,
[
23
, Section 8]
,
and
[
67
, Sections 0.3.2 and 1.3.1]
;
see
[
67
, Section 0.3.10]
for some further references.

Before defining semicontramodules, let us recall from the discussion
in Section
1.1
that there are two ways to define
the conventional modules over associative algebras over
k
in
tensor/polylinear algebra terms.
In addition to the familiar definition of a left
A
-module
M

as a
k
-vector space endowed with a
k
-linear map

n\colon A\otimes_{k}M\allowbreak\longrightarrow M
satisfying the associativity and
unitality equations, one can also say that a left
A
-module
structure on
M
is defined by a linear map
p\colon M\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}(A,M)

satisfying the correspodingly rewritten equations.

A
left semicontramodule

{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}
over a semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
over
a coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}
is an object of the opposite category to
the category of module objects in the right module category

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}^{\mathsf{op}}
over the ring object
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
in the tensor category

{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod--}}}{\mathcal{C}}
.
In other words, it is a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule endowed with
a
left semicontraaction
map
{\mathbf{p}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}\longrightarrow\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})

satisfying the following
contralinearity
,

semicontraassociativity
, and
semicontraunitality

equations.
First of all, the map
{\mathbf{p}}
must be a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
morphism.
Secondly, the compositions of the semicontraaction map
{\mathbf{p}}

with the two maps
\Cohom({\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\colon\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\longrightarrow\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\medspace{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})
and
\Cohom({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\mathbf{p}})\colon\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\longrightarrow\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}))

{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}\longrightarrow\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\rightrightarrows\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\medspace{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\simeq\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}))

should be equal to each other,
\Cohom({\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\circ{\mathbf{p}}=\Cohom({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\mathbf{p}})\circ{\mathbf{p}}
, where the above identification

\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\medspace{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\simeq\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}))

is presumed.
Thirdly, the composition of the semicontraaction map with
the map
\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathbf{e}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\colon\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}

induced by the semiunit map
{\mathbf{e}}

{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}\longrightarrow\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}

should be equal to the identity map,
\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathbf{e}},{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}})\circ{\mathbf{p}}={\mathrm{id}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
This definition can be found in
[
67
, Sections 0.3.5
or 3.3.1]
.

For any right
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semimodule
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
and any
k
-vector space
V
,
the left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
\Hom_{k}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}},V)
has a natural left

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semicontramodule structure.
The left semicontraaction map
{\mathbf{p}}\colon\Hom_{k}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}},V)\longrightarrow\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\Hom_{k}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}},V))
is constructed by applying the functor

\Hom_{k}({-},V)
to the right semiaction map
{\mathbf{n}}
of
the
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semimodule
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

\Hom_{k}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}},V)\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\medspace V)\simeq\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\Hom_{k}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}},V)).

Generally speaking, the kernels of arbitrary morphisms exist in
the category of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semimodules
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
and are preserved
by the forgetful functors
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--vect}}}
,
but the cokernels in
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
may be problematic when
{\mathcal{C}}
is not
an injective right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule.
Similarly, the cokernels of arbitrary morphisms exist in
the category of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semicontramodules
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}
and are
preserved by the forgetful functors
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--vect}}}
, but the kernels in
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}
may be problematic
when
{\mathcal{C}}
is not an injective left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule.

Now let us assume that the semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
is an injective
right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule.
Then the cotensor product functor
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{-}\colon{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
is exact, so the category
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
of left

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semimodules is abelian and the forgetful functors

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--vect}}}
are exact.
Both the infinite direct sums and infinite products exist in

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
and both are preserved by the forgetful functor

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
, though only the infinite direct sums
are preserved by the full forgetful functor
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--vect}}}
.

Indeed, let
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}_{\alpha}
be a family of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semimodules and

\prod_{\alpha}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}_{\alpha}
be their infinite product in the category
of left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
; then one can easily construct
a left semiaction map
{\mathbf{m}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}\prod_{\alpha}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}_{\alpha}\longrightarrow\prod_{\alpha}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}_{\alpha}
and show that the left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semimodule so
obtained is the product of the family of objects
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}_{\alpha}
in

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
.
So the category
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
satisfies the axioms Ab5 and Ab3*, but
not in general Ab4*.
It also has a set of generators, for which one can take
the
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semimodules
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}
induced from finite-dimensional
left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules
{\mathcal{L}}
.
Hence there are enough injective objects in
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
; we will see
in Section
3.5
below how one can construct them.

Furthermore, the vector space
{\mathfrak{A}}=\Hom_{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}})=\Hom_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}}({\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}})
can be endowed with a topological
k
-algebra
structure (with a base of neighborhoods of zero formed by open
left ideals) such that the category of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semimodules is
isomorphic to the category of discrete left
{\mathfrak{A}}
-modules,

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}\simeq{\mathfrak{A}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--discr}}}

[
90
, Remark 10.9]
.
This provides another point of view on the categorical properties
of the category
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
.

Assume that the semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
is an injective left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule.
Then the cohomomorphism functor
\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{-})\colon{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
is exact, so the category
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}
of left

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semicontramodules is abelian and the forgetful functors

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--vect}}}
are exact.
Both the infinite direct sums and infinite products exist in
the category
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}
and both are preserved by the forgetful functor

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
, though only the infinite products are
preserved by the full forgetful functor
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--vect}}}
.

Indeed, let
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}_{\alpha}
be a family of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semicontramodules
and
\bigoplus_{\alpha}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}_{\alpha}
be their infinite direct sum in
the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
.
Then one can easily construct a left semicontraaction map

{\mathbf{p}}:\bigoplus_{\alpha}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}_{\alpha}\longrightarrow\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},\medspace\bigoplus_{\alpha}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}_{\alpha})

and show that the left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semicontramodule so obtained is
the direct sum of the family of objects
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}_{\alpha}
in
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}
.
So the category
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}
satisfies the axioms Ab3 and Ab4*,
but not in general Ab4 or Ab5*.
There are enough projective objects in
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}
; we will see
in Section
3.5
how to construct them.

Furthermore, the vector space
{\mathfrak{R}}=\Hom_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}})=\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}})

can be endowed with a topological
k
-algebra structure (with a base of neighborhoods of zero formed by open right ideals) such that
the category of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semicontramodules is
isomorphic to the category of left
{\mathfrak{R}}
-contramodules,

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--sicntr}}}\simeq{\mathfrak{R}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}

[
90
, Proposition 10.8 and subsequent
discussion]
(see also Section
3.7
below).
This provides another point of view on the categorical properties
of the category
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
, including a description of its
projective objects.

Example
.

Let us explain the construction of the semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
for which
the category of
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
-semicontramodules is equivalent to
the category of contramodules over a (locally compact totally
disconnected) topological group
G
, as it was promised in
Section
1.8
.
In fact, we will see that for any given group
G
there is a whole
family of such semialgebras
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
depending on the choice of
a compact (i. e., profinite) open subgroup
H\subset G
.
All of them are Morita equivalent to each other in the sense
of
[
67
, Section 8.4.5]
, i. e., the categories of
(say, left) semimodules over all of them are equivalent, as are
the categories of semicontramodules.

Given a commutative ring
k
, by a
discrete
G
-module
over
k
we mean a
k
-module
{\mathcal{M}}
endowed with a
k
-linear
discrete
G
-module structure
{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}\{G\}
; similarly,
a
G
-contramodule over
k
is a
k
-module endowed
with a
k
-linear
G
-contramodule structure
{\mathfrak{P}}[[G]]\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}
.
In other words, a discrete
G
-module over
k
is a
k
-linear
object in the additive category
G{\operatorname{\mathsf{--discr}}}
and a
G
-contramodule
over
k
is a
k
-linear object in the additive category
G{\operatorname{\mathsf{--contra}}}
.

For the beginning, let
k
be a field.
We will freely use the terminology and notation of
Section
1.8
; in particular,
k(X)
denotes
the vector space of locally constant compactly supported

k
-valued functions on a (locally compact totally disconnected)
topological space
X
.
Then for any topological spaces
X
and
Y
there is a natural
isomorphism
k(X\times Y)\simeq k(X)\otimes_{k}k(Y)
.
For any profinite group
H
, the inverse image map

k(H)\longrightarrow k(H\times H)
with respect to the multiplication
map
H\times H\longrightarrow H
, together with the map
k(H)\longrightarrow k

of evaluation at the unit element
e\in H
, endow
the vector space
k(H)
with the structure of a coassociative
coalgebra over
k
.
For any
k
-vector space
A
one has
A\{H\}=A(H)\simeq k(H)\otimes_{k}A

and
A[[H]]\simeq\Hom_{k}(k(H),A)
, so one can easily identify
discrete
H
-modules over
k
with (left or right)
k(H)
-comodules
and
H
-contramodules over
k
with
k(H)
-contramodules.

Let
H
be a compact open subgroup in a topological group
G
;
then the left and right actions of
H
in
G
endow
k(G)
with
a natural structure of bicomodule over
k(H)
.
Denote by
G\times^{H}G
the quotient space of the Cartesian square

G\times G
by the equivalence relation
(g^{\prime}h,g^{\prime\prime})\sim(g^{\prime},hg^{\prime\prime})

for all
g^{\prime}
,
g^{\prime\prime}\in G
and
h\in H
.
Being a disjoint union of
G/H
copies of
G
, this quotient
is also a locally compact and totally disconnected topological space.
The inverse image of functions with respect to the factorization
map
G\times G\longrightarrow G\times^{H}G
identifies the vector space

k(G\times^{H}G)
with the cotensor product
k(G)\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{k(H)}k(G)\subset k(G)\otimes_{k}k(G)=k(G\times G)
.

For any étale map (local homeomorphism) of topological spaces

p\colon X\longrightarrow Y
and any abelian group
A
, the push-forward map

A(X)\longrightarrow A(Y)
, assigning to a function
f\colon X\longrightarrow A

the function
p_{*}(f)\colon Y\longrightarrow A
,

(p_{*}f)(y)=\sum_{p(x)=y}f(x),\qquad y\in Y,\ \ x\in X,

is defined
[
67
, Section E.1.1]
.
In particular, the push-forwards with respect to the multiplication
map
G\times^{H}G\longrightarrow G
and the embedding map
H\longrightarrow G
provide
natural left and right
H
-equivariant
k
-linear maps

k(G)\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{k(H)}k(G)\longrightarrow k(G)
and
k(H)\longrightarrow k(G)
endowing
the vector space
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)=k(G)
with the structure of a semialgebra
over the coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(H)=k(H)

[
67
, Section E.1.2]
.

It is claimed that the category of (left or right)

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
-semimodules is isomorphic to the category of
discrete
G
-modules over
k
; the datum of a
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
-semimodule
structure on a
k
-vector space
{\mathcal{M}}
is equivalent to the datum of
a discrete
G
-module structure on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Indeed, according to
[
67
, Sections E.1.3 and 10.2.2]
,
the datum of a
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
-semimodule structure on
{\mathcal{M}}
is equivalent
to that of two structures of a
G
-module and a
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(H)
-comodule
satisfying two compatibility equations; essentially, this is the same
as an action of
G
in
{\mathcal{M}}
whose restriction to
H
comes from
a
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(H)
-coaction.
It remains to notice that an action of
G
whose restriction to
H

is discrete is the same thing as a discrete action of
G
.

Similarly, the category of
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
-semicontramodules is isomorphic
to the category of
G
-contramodules over
k
; the datum of
a
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
-semicontramodule structure on a
k
-vector space
{\mathfrak{P}}

is equivalent to the datum of a
G
-contramodule structure.
Indeed, according to
loc. cit.
, the datum of
a
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
-semicontramodule structure on
{\mathcal{M}}
is equivalent to
that of two structures of a
G
-module and a
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(H)
-contramodule
satisfying two compatibility equations.
Essentially, it is claimed that a contraassociative
G
-contraction
map
{\mathfrak{P}}[[G]]\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}
can be uniquely recovered from its restriction
to the point measures in
G
and the measures supported inside
H
,
provided that the two compatibility equations are satisfied.
One notices that there is an external product map
k[[G]]\otimes_{k}{\mathfrak{P}}[[G]]\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}[[G\times G]]
or
{\mathfrak{P}}[[G]]\otimes_{k}k[[G]]\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}[[G\times G]]
assiging to a
k
-valued and a
{\mathfrak{P}}
-valued
measures on
G
a
{\mathfrak{P}}
-valued measure on
G\times G
.
The contraassociativity equation in the definition of
a
G
-contramodule, restricted to the external products of

k
-valued point measures in
G
and
{\mathfrak{P}}
-valued measures in
H
,
taken in any fixed order, provides a prescription for the desired
recovering of the
G
-contraaction map from its restrictions to
the two specific kinds of measures (cf.
[
78
, proof of
Corollary 3.1]
).

Notice that the underlying
k
-vector space
k(G)
of
the semialgebras
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
does not depend on the choice of
a compact open subgroup
H\subset G
, but the semialgebra structure
does,
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
being a semialgebra over the coalgebra

{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(H)=k(H)
depending on
H
.
Still, the abelian categories of semimodules and semicontramodules
over
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
do not depend on
H
; but their semiderived
categories and the functors of semi-infinite (co)homology and
the derived semimodule-semicontramodule correspondence, whose
construction is the aim of the book
[
67
]
,
do
depend
on
H
in a quite essential way
[
67
, Section 8.4.6
and Remark E.3.2]
.

Now one would like to replace a field
k
with an arbitrary
commutative ring, including first of all
k={\mathbb{Z}}
.
This was one of the motivating examples for developing the theory
of semimodules and semicontramodules in the generality of
semialgebras over corings over (generally speaking, noncommutative)
rings rather than just over coalgebras over fields in the main
body of the book
[
67
]
.
One unpleasant technical complication that arises in this
connection is the possible nonassociativity of cotensor product
over a coring (see the discussion in the beginning of
Section
2.5
).
Hence the importance of various sufficient conditions guaranteeing
such associativity; see
[
22
, Sections 11.6 and 22.5–6]

and
[
67
, Section 1.2.5]
.

In particular, the results of
[
22
, 11.6(iv)]

or
[
67
, Proposition 1.2.5(f)]
ensure that the notions of
a semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)
and arbitrary semimodules over it are
unproblematic for any commutative ring
k
.
To consider semicontramodules, one also needs associativity
of the cohomomorphism functor, which holds in this case by
the result of
[
67
, Proposition 3.2.5(j)]
.
All the assertions mentioned above in this example still hold in
this setting.
The more advanced homological constructions and results
of
[
67
]
in the application to the semialgebras
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}_{k}(G,H)

depend on the assumption of the ring
k
having finite
homological dimension, though.

2.7.
The category
{\mathsf{O}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}

The conventional concept of representations of an algebraic group

G
is that of comodules over the coalgebra of regular
functions
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
on
G
.
Since every comodule over a coassociative coalgebra is the union
of its finite-dimensional subcomodules
[
102
, Section 2.1]

(cf. the discussion in
Sections
1.3
–
1.4
), it means
that infinite-dimensional representations of
G
are simply
the unions of finite-dimensional representations, or
the
ind-finite-dimensional
representations.

In particular, while every finite-dimensional representation
of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of a simply connected semisimple complex
algebraic group
G
can be integrated to a representation of
G
,
this is
no
longer true for infinite-dimensional representations.
Indeed, for any nonzero Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
one can easily construct
a module that is not a union of its finite-dimensional submodules
(it suffices to consider the enveloping algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})
with
the action of
{\mathfrak{g}}
by left multiplications).

The Lie correspondence takes a particularly simple form in the case
of unipotent algebraic groups and nilpotent Lie algebras over
a field of characteristic zero, which are two equivalent
categories
[
29
, Corollaire VI.2.4.5]

(see
[
67
, Section D.6.1]
for further references and
a discussion including the finite characteristic case).
A module over a finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}

comes from an (always unique) representation of the corresponding
unipotent algebraic group
G
if and only if it is a union of
finite-dimensional
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules where all the vectors from
{\mathfrak{g}}

act by nilpotent linear operators
[
63
, Sections 3.3.5–7]
.

It is a classical idea to work with categories intermediate between
those of representations of a Lie (or algebraic) group
G
and modules
over its Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
For this purpose, one starts from a Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
with
a chosen
subgroup

H
, i. e., an algebraic group corresponding
to a Lie subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then one considers
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules
{\mathcal{M}}
for which the restriction to

{\mathfrak{h}}
of the action of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in
{\mathcal{M}}
can be/has been integrated to
an algebraic action of
H
.
As to the choice of the subgroup
H
, there are two basic
approaches: given a complex (or real) semisimple Lie group
G
and
its Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, one can take
H
to be a maximal compact
subgroup of
G
; or otherwise one can use a Borel (or maximal
unipotent) subgroup of
G
in the role of
H
.

Modules over a semisimple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
integrable to
representations of a maximal compact Lie subgroup
H
are classically
known as
Harish-Chandra modules

[
30
,
105
]
,
while
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules which can be integrated to an algebraic action of
the Borel subgroup form what has been called
the
BGG
(Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand)
category
{\mathsf{O}}

[
11
,
47
]
.
Both can be united under an umbrella notion of
algebraic
Harish-Chandra modules
, which means simply “modules over a pair
consisting of a Lie algebra and an algebraic subgroup”
(see a terminological discussion in
[
67
, Remark D.2.5]
).

An
algebraic Harish-Chandra
pair

[
12
, Sections 1.8.2 and 3.3.2]
is a set of data consisting
of a Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over a field
k
, a finite-dimensional Lie
subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset H
, an algebraic group
H
over
k
whose Lie
algebra is identified with
{\mathfrak{h}}
, and an action of
H
by automorphisms
of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The following two compatibility conditions have to be satisfied.
Firstly, the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}
must be an
H
-invariant
subspace in
{\mathfrak{g}}
, and the restriction of the action of
H
in
{\mathfrak{g}}

to
{\mathfrak{h}}
should coincide with the adjoint action of
H
in
{\mathfrak{h}}
.
Secondly, the action of
{\mathfrak{h}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
obtained by taking the derivative
of the action of
H
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
should coincide with the adjoint action
of
{\mathfrak{h}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

A
Harish-Chandra module

{\mathcal{M}}
over an algebraic Harish-Chandra
pair
({\mathfrak{g}},H)
is a
k
-vector space endowed with a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module
structure and an action of
H
satisfying the following compatibility
conditions.
Firstly, the restriction of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-action in
{\mathcal{M}}
to the Lie
subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}
should coincide with the derivative of the action
of
H
in
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Secondly, the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-action map
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}
should be

H
-equivariant.

In algebraic (rather than algebro-geometric) terms, an (affine)
algebraic group
G
over a field
k
is described by
the
k
-vector space
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
of regular functions on
G
,
endowed with a noncommutative convolution comultiplication

\mu\colon{\mathcal{C}}(G)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}(G)\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{C}}(G)
induced by the multiplication map

G\times G\longrightarrow G
and a commutative pointwise multiplication

m\colon{\mathcal{C}}(G)\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{C}}(G)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}(G)
.
Together with the antipode map
s\colon{\mathcal{C}}(G)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}(G)
induced
by the inverse element map
G\longrightarrow G
, these structures make

{\mathcal{C}}(G)
a commutative Hopf algebra
[
102
]
.
By a representation of
G
one conventionally means
a
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
-comodule, while the multiplication on
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
is being
used in order to define a
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
-comodule structure on
the tensor product
{\mathcal{L}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}
of any two
k
-vector spaces
{\mathcal{L}}

and
{\mathcal{M}}
endowed with
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
-comodule structures.
The antipode map
s
, being (always) an anti-automorphism for (both
the multiplication and) the comultiplication of
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}(G)
(or any
other Hopf algebra
{\mathcal{C}}
), allows to identify the categories of left
and right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodules, so the difference between them is
inessential here.

Over a field
k
of characteristic
0
, the enveloping algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})

of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of an algebraic group
G
is interpreted as
the algebra of left or right invariant differential operators on
G

or, simpler yet, the algebra of distributions (“delta functions”)
on
G
supported at the unit element
e\in G
.
The
k
-vector space of distributions here is defined as the discrete
dual vector space to the linearly compact
k
-vector space of functions
on the formal completion of
G
at
e
.
The noncommutative convolution multiplication
m\colon U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})
in the Hopf algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})
is induced by the multiplication map

G\times G\longrightarrow G
, while the commutative comultiplication

\mu\colon U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}U({\mathfrak{g}})
is induced by the diagonal embedding

G\longrightarrow G\times G
, and the antipode map
s\colon U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})

simply multiplies every vector from
{\mathfrak{g}}
by
-1
.

Evaluating a
\{e\}
-supported distribution at a regular function
on
G
defines a natural pairing
\phi\colon{\mathcal{C}}(G)\otimes_{k}U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow k
.
For example, the pairing with an element of
{\mathfrak{g}}
assigns to
a regular function on
G
the value of its derivative along
the corresponding tangent vector at the origin
e\in G
.
The pairing
\phi
is compatible with the Hopf algebra structures
on
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
and
U({\mathfrak{g}})
, transforming the comultiplication in
the former into the multiplication in the latter and vice versa.
In our left-right conventions (see Section
1.4
for
a discussion), this compatibility is expressed by the equations

\displaystyle\phi(f,uv)

\displaystyle=\phi(f_{(2)},u)\phi(f_{(1)},v)

\displaystyle\phi(fg,u)

\displaystyle=\phi(f,u_{(2)})\phi(g,u_{(1)}),

for any
f
,
g\in{\mathcal{C}}(G)
and
u
,
v\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
,
where
\mu(f)=f_{(1)}\otimes f_{(2)}
and
\mu(u)=u_{(1)}\otimes u_{(2)}

is Sweedler’s symbolic notation for the comultiplication maps.

Given a
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
, one defines the “derivative”

U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module structure
m\colon U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}
on
{\mathcal{M}}

as given by the composition
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{C}}(G)\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}
of the maps induced by
the coaction map and the pairing
\phi

(with the arguments’ positions inverted).
Alternatively, one can obtain a left action map in the form

p\colon{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}(U(g),{\mathcal{M}})
as the composition

{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}(U({\mathfrak{g}}),{\mathcal{M}})
of the left coaction
map and the map induced by the pairing
\phi
.
Furthermore, the adjoint action of
G
in itself preserves the origin,
so,
unlike
the left and right actions of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in the enveloping
algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})
, the adjoint action of
{\mathfrak{g}}
can be integrated to
a representation of
G
in
U({\mathfrak{g}})
as well as in
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Hence both
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
U({\mathfrak{g}})
are endowed with natural
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
-comodule
structures.

Now an algebraic Harish-Chandra pair
({\mathfrak{g}},H)
is described in purely
algebraic terms as a set of data consisting of a Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
a Hopf algebra
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
, a Lie subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
, a pairing

\phi\colon{\mathcal{C}}(H)\otimes_{k}U({\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow k
compatible with the Hopf algebra
structures, and a coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfying the following
compatibility conditions.
Firstly, the coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
should be compatible with
the Lie algebra structure on
{\mathfrak{g}}
; then there is also the induced
coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
in
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
Secondly, the Lie subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
should be preserved by
the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-coaction and the pairing
\phi
should be compatible
with the induced coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
in
U({\mathfrak{h}})
and the adjoint
coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
in itself (equivalently, the restriction

\psi\colon{\mathcal{C}}(H)\times{\mathfrak{h}}\longrightarrow k
of the pairing
\phi
should be
compatible with the adjoint
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-coaction in
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
and

{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-coaction in
{\mathfrak{g}}
restricted to
{\mathfrak{h}}
).
Thirdly, the adjoint action of
{\mathfrak{h}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
should coincide with
the derivative
{\mathfrak{h}}
-action of the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-coaction, which is
defined in terms of the given pairing
\phi
.

Two generalizations of this setting, in two different directions,
are discussed at length in the book
[
67
]
.
A “quantum” version, with two noncommutative, noncocommutative
Hopf algebras
{\mathcal{C}}
and
K
in place of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
and
U({\mathfrak{h}})
,
an associative algebra
R
in the role of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
, and “adjoint”
coactions of
{\mathcal{C}}
in
K
and
R
, is introduced
in
[
67
, Section C.1]
.
A “Tate” version, with the Hopf algebra
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
of regular functions
on an infinite-dimensional pro-affine pro-algebraic group
H
and
a linearly compact open subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}
in a Tate (locally linearly
compact) Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, can be found in
[
67
, Section D.2.1]

(see the overview in Section
2.8
below).

To repeat a previously given definition in the slightly new language,
a Harish-Chandra module
{\mathcal{M}}
over an algebraic Harish-Chandra pair

({\mathfrak{g}},H)
is a
k
-vector space endowed with a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module and
a
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodule structures satisfying two compatibility conditions.
The restriction of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-action in
{\mathcal{M}}
to
{\mathfrak{h}}
should coincide
with the derivative
{\mathfrak{h}}
-action of the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-coaction, and
the action map
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}
should be a
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodule
morphism (where the coaction in the tensor product is defined
in terms of the multiplication in the Hopf algebra
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
);
equivalently, the action map
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}
should be
a
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodule morphism.

Before defining
Harish-Chandra contramodules
, let us say
a few words about contramodules over the coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
of
regular functions on an algebraic group
G
.
Unfortunately, there does
not
seem to be any particular way
to
interpret
a
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
-contramodule structure on
a
k
-vector space
{\mathfrak{P}}
in any terms more explicit than the general
definition of a contramodule over a coalgebra over a field
k
.
The only known exception is the case of a reductive algebraic
group
H
over a field
k
of characteristic
0
, when

[
67
, Lemma A.2.2]
or the last sentence of
Section
1.2
apply and the semisimple abelian
categories of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodules and
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodules
are equivalent.
Otherwise there is only the general intuition of contramodules as
modules with infinite summation operations, supported by examples
such as that of comodules and contramodules over the coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}(H)

of regular functions on the one-dimensional unipotent algebraic
group
H=\mathbb{G}_{a}
considered in Section
1.3
.

However, the vector spaces
{\mathfrak{P}}=\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{M}},V)
for all

{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodules
{\mathcal{M}}
(i. e., vector spaces with the algebraic
group
H
acting in them in the conventional sense)
and all
k
-vector spaces
V
provide
examples
of

{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodules for
any
algebraic group
H

(see Section
1.2
).
Moreover, for any commutative (for simplicity) Hopf algebra
{\mathcal{C}}
,
a
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
, and a
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}
, the
k
-vector
space
{\mathfrak{Q}}=\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathfrak{P}})
has a natural
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule structure.
To construct the desired left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contraaction map
\pi_{\mathfrak{Q}}\colon\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{C}},\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathfrak{P}}))\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathfrak{P}})
, suppose that
we are given a
k
-linear map
g\colon{\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathfrak{P}})

and a vector
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
Consider the
k
-linear map
f\colon{\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}
assigning to
any element
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
the vector

f(c)=g\big(s(m_{(-1)})c\big)(m_{(0)})\in{\mathfrak{P}},

and set

\pi_{\mathfrak{Q}}(g)=\pi_{\mathfrak{P}}(f),

where
m\longmapsto m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)}
is the Sweedler notation for
the left coaction map
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}\colon{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{M}}
, and

\pi_{\mathfrak{P}}\colon\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathfrak{P}})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}
is the left contraaction map of
the original
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}

[
67
, Section C.4.2]
.

Furthermore, given an algebraic group
G
and a
{\mathcal{C}}(G)
-contramodule

{\mathfrak{P}}
, one defines the “contraderivative”
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module structure

U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}{\mathfrak{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}
on
{\mathfrak{P}}
as given by the composition

U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}{\mathfrak{P}}\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}({\mathcal{C}}(G),{\mathfrak{P}})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{P}}
of the map

u\otimes p\longmapsto(c\mapsto\phi(c,u)p),\qquad u\in U({\mathfrak{g}}),\ \,c\in{\mathcal{C}}(G),\ \,p\in{\mathfrak{P}}

induced by the pairing
\phi\colon{\mathcal{C}}(G)\otimes_{k}U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow k
with
the left contraaction map
\pi

(cf.
[
67
, Sections 10.1.2]
).

A
Harish-Chandra contramodule

{\mathfrak{P}}
over an algebraic
Harish-Chandra pair
({\mathfrak{g}},H)
is a
k
-vector space endowed with
a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module and a
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodule structures satisfying
the following two compatibility conditions.
Firstly, the restriction of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module structure on
{\mathfrak{P}}

to the Lie subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
should coincide with
the contraderivative
{\mathfrak{h}}
-module structure of
the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodule structure on
{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Secondly, the
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-action map in the form

{\mathfrak{P}}\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}(U({\mathfrak{g}}),{\mathfrak{P}})
should be a
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodule morphism,
where the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodule structure on
\Hom_{k}(U({\mathfrak{g}}),{\mathfrak{P}})
is
obtained from the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodule structure on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
and
the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodule structure on
{\mathfrak{P}}
as described above.

The latter condition is equivalent to the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-action map

{\mathfrak{P}}\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{P}})
being a
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodule morphism.
To convince oneself that this is so, one can present the space

\Hom_{k}(U({\mathfrak{g}}),{\mathfrak{P}})
as the projective limit of the Hom spaces

\Hom_{k}(F_{n}U({\mathfrak{g}}),{\mathfrak{P}})
, where
F
denotes the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt
filtration of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
, and further present every space

\Hom_{k}(F_{n}U({\mathfrak{g}}),{\mathfrak{P}})
as a subspace of the Hom space

\Hom_{k}(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes n},\medspace{\mathfrak{P}})
.
Then it remains to use the fact that the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodule structure
on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
is compatible with the associative algebra structure, i. e.,
the multiplication map
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{k}U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})
is
a
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodule morphism, together with the assumption of
associativity of the
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-action in
{\mathfrak{P}}
.

Viewing the case of a semisimple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
with a Borel or
maximal unipotent subgroup
H
as our main example, we denote by

{\mathsf{O}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
the category of Harish-Chandra modules over
an algebraic Harish-Chandra pair
({\mathfrak{g}},H)
and by
{\mathsf{O}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)

the category of Harish-Chandra contramodules.
These are abelian categories with exact forgetful functors

{\mathsf{O}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--vect}}}
and
{\mathsf{O}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--vect}}}
,
the former of which preserves infinite direct sums, while
the latter preserves infinite products.
The category
{\mathsf{O}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
satisfies the axioms Ab5 and Ab3* (but
not Ab4*), while the category
{\mathsf{O}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
satisfies
the axioms Ab3 and Ab4* (but not Ab4 or Ab5*).

Now it is claimed that there is a semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
over
the coalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}(H)
such that the categories
{\mathsf{O}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)

and
{\mathsf{O}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
are identified with the categories of
semimodules and semicontramodules over
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}
.
More precisely, there are naturally
two
such semialgebras

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
, differing by the left-right symmetry.
The category of Harish-Chandra modules
{\mathsf{O}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
is isomorphic
to the categories of left semimodules over
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
and
right semimodules over
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
, while the category of
Harish-Chandra contramodules
{\mathsf{O}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
is isomorphic
to the category of left semicontramodules over
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
.

The semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
is constructed as the tensor product

U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}(H)
, where the left
U({\mathfrak{h}})
-module structure
on
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
is obtained by deriving the left coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)

in itself.
The right
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule structure on
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}(H)
is
induced by the right coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}
in itself, while the left

{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule structure on this tensor product is defined in
terms of the multiplication in
{\mathcal{C}}
, as the tensor product of
the left coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}
in itself and the adjoint coaction of

{\mathcal{C}}
in
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
The semiunit map
{\mathbf{e}}\colon{\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}=U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}
is induced
by the embedding of algebras
U({\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
Finally, the semimultiplication map
{\mathbf{m}}\colon{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}\longrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}

is defined
as the composition

\big(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}\big)\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}\big(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}\big)\text{$\mskip 1.5mu$}\simeq\text{$\mskip 1.5mu$}U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}\big({\mathcal{C}}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}})\big)\\
\text{$\mskip 1.5mu$}\simeq\text{$\mskip 1.5mu$}U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}\text{$\mskip 1.5mu$}\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\text{$\mskip 1.5mu$}U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}

of the mutual associativity isomorphism of the tensor and cotensor
products, whose natural existence in this case can be easily
established from the fact that
U({\mathfrak{g}})
is a projective right

U({\mathfrak{h}})
-module (by the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt theorem)

[
67
, Section 1.2.3]
, and the map induced by
the multiplication map
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
This construction can be found in
[
67
, Section 10.2.1]
.

Notice that for any left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule
{\mathcal{M}}
there is a natural
isomorphism

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{M}}=\big(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}\big)\mathbin{\text{\smaller$\square$}}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{M}}\simeq U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{M}},

where the
U({\mathfrak{h}})
-module structure on
{\mathcal{M}}
is obtained by deriving
the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodule structure.
Hence it follows that the datum of a left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}
-semimodule
structure on a
k
-vector space
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
is equivalent to that of
a left
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-comodule and a left
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module structures
on
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
satisfying two compatibility
equations
[
67
, Section 10.2.2]
.
These are easily seen to express the definition of a structure of
Harish-Chandra module over
({\mathfrak{g}},H)
on the vector space
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
.

Similarly, the semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}={\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
is constructed as
the tensor product
{\mathcal{C}}(H)\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}U({\mathfrak{g}})
, where the right

U({\mathfrak{h}})
-module structure on
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
is obtained by deriving
the right coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
in itself.
The left
{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule structure on this tensor product is
induced by the left coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}
in itself, while the right

{\mathcal{C}}
-comodule structure is obtained by multiplying
the right coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}
in itself and the adjoint
coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}
in
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
The semimultiplication and semiunit maps of the semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}

are induced by the multiplication map
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})
and the embedding
U({\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})
, as above.

For any left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule
{\mathfrak{P}}
there is a natural isomorphism

\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r},{\mathfrak{P}})=\Cohom_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{C}}\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}U({\mathfrak{g}}),\medspace{\mathfrak{P}})\simeq\Hom_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}(U({\mathfrak{g}}),{\mathfrak{P}}),

where the
U({\mathfrak{h}})
-module structure on
{\mathfrak{P}}
is obtained by
contraderiving the
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
-contramodule structure.
Hence one concludes that the datum of a left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
-semicontramodule
structure on a
k
-vector spaced
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}
is equivalent to that of
a left
{\mathcal{C}}
-contramodule and left
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module structures on
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}

satisfying two compatibility equations (cf. Example in
Section
2.6
).
These are easily seen to express the definition of a structure of
Harish-Chandra contramodule over
({\mathfrak{g}},H)
on the vector space
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

In addition to these descriptions of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}
-semimodules and
left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
-semicontramodules, one would like to have an explicit
interpretation of what it means to have a left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
-semimodule
structure on a
k
-vector space
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}
.
Such a description of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
-semimodules is indeed obtained
in
[
67
, Sections C.2 and C.4.3–4]
under certain mild
assumptions, which we will now discuss.

So far we used the notions of an “algebraic group
H
”
and “a commutative Hopf algebra
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
” interchangeably, but
in fact there are several differences between the two
(cf.
[
28
]
).
First of all, it is only
affine
algebraic groups that
can be described by the algebras of global functions on them.
As our aim is to consider linear representations of our algebraic
groups, we can safely assume all of them to be affine.

Secondly, it is only
finitely generated
commutative
algebras over a field that correspond to algebraic varieties;
the spectrum of an arbitrary commutative algebra is, generally
speaking, a
pro-affine pro-algebraic variety
.
Considering Harish-Chandra pairs with pro-affine pro-algebraic
groups
H
presumes also having an infinite-dimensional
linearly compact Lie subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Postponing this discussion to Section
2.8
,
we for now assume all our algebraic groups
H
to be
finite-dimensional, or the commutative Hopf algebras
{\mathcal{C}}(H)

to be finitely generated as algebras over
k
.

Thirdly and finally,
algebraic varieties
are generally
assumed to be
reduced schemes
, i. e., to have no nilpotent
elements in their structure sheaves (or, if they are affine, in
the algebras of global functions on them).
Now, over a field
k
of characteristic
0
, every algebraic

group
scheme is a smooth variety, and over any field
k

every reduced algebraic group scheme is a smooth variety; but
over a field
k
of finite characteristic there exist

nonreduced
algebraic group schemes.
It suffices to consider the spectrum of the finite-dimensional
algebra
{\mathcal{C}}=k[x]/(x^{p})
over a field
k
of characteristic
p

and notice that the rule
\mu(x)=1\otimes x+x\otimes 1
describes
a well-defined coassociative, counital comultiplication making

{\mathcal{C}}
a Hopf algebra over
k
.

Now, assuming the algebraic group
H
to be a smooth
finite-dimensional variety over a field
k
, there is
the one-dimensional vector bundle of differential forms of
the top degree on
H
.
The group
H
acts in itself by the left and right multiplications,
and there are the two induced actions in the vector space of
global differential top forms
{\mathcal{E}}=\Omega^{top}(H)
.
The subspace of left
H
-invariant top forms in
{\mathcal{E}}
is always
one-dimensional, as is the subspace of right
H
-invariant top
forms, but these two subspaces may not coincide.
A smooth algebraic group
H
admitting a nonzero
biinvariant

top form is said to be
unimodular
.
An algebraic group
H
is unimodular if and only if all
the operators of its adjoint representation
\Ad_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow\GL({\mathfrak{h}})

belong to the subgroup
\SL({\mathfrak{h}})\subset\GL({\mathfrak{h}})
.
All the reductive algebraic groups are unimodular, as are all
the nilpotent ones; but many solvable groups are not.

Let us first assume the smooth algebraic group
H
to be unimodular.
Then the semialgebras
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}({\mathfrak{g}},H)
are naturally
isomorphic to each other; the isomorphism is provided by the maps
given by the formulas

c\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}u\longmapsto u_{[0]}\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}cu_{[1]}\quad\text{and}\quad u\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}c\longmapsto s(u_{[1]})c\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}u_{[0]},

where
c\in{\mathcal{C}}(H)
,
u\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
, the notation
c\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}u

and
u\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}c
stands for elements of
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}={\mathcal{C}}(H)\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}U({\mathfrak{g}})

and
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}=U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}{\mathcal{C}}(H)
, respectively, and

u\longmapsto u_{[0]}\otimes u_{[1]}
with
u_{[0]}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
and

u_{[1]}\in{\mathcal{C}}(H)
is the Sweedler notation for right coaction
map defining the adjoint coaction of
{\mathcal{C}}(H)
in
U({\mathfrak{g}})

[
67
, Section C.2.6]
.
Accordingly, the categories of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
-semimodules and
left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}
-semimodules are naturally isomorphic.

In the general case of a nonunimodular smooth algebraic group
H
,
the categories of left
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
-semimodules and left

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}
-semimodules are still naturally equivalent, but
the equivalence
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}\simeq{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}
does
not
commute
with the forgetful functors
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
and

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--simod}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
.
Instead, the two forgetful functors differ by a twist with
the modular character
\det\Ad_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow GL({\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow k^{*}

[
67
, Sections C.2.4–5]
.

When the ground field
k
has characteristic
0

and the Harish-Chandra pair
({\mathfrak{g}},H)
originates from a closed
embedding of algebraic groups
H\subset G
over
k
, choosing
a biinvariant top form on
H
allows to interpret elements of
the semialgebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}\simeq{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
as distributions on the smooth
variety
G
, supported in the smooth closed subvariety
H
and
regular along
H

[
67
, Remark C.4.4]
.
In the nonunimodular case, the
k
-vector space of all
distributions on
G
, supported in
H
and regular along
H
,
has a natural structure of an
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}
-
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
-bisemimodule

providing the Morita equivalence between the semialgebras

{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{l}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}}^{r}
.

2.8.
Tate Harish-Chandra pairs

It appears that one cannot integrate the Virasoro Lie algebra to
a Lie group, but
a half
of it one easily can.
Indeed, let
k
be a field of characteristic
0
.
Denote by
H(k)
